========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it's... I suppose it's a bit of a story. You see, I've been helping out down at the community center for, oh, must be fifteen years now. Ever since I retired, really. Mostly I just help set up for the Sunday luncheons, bingo nights, things of that nature. I like to keep busy, you know? A man my age needs a reason to put his shoes on in the morning and get out of the house. 

Anyway, last week we were getting the hall ready, and there's this new kid, one of the younger volunteers. Good kid, I suppose, but just doesn't pay attention to the details, always looking at his phone. I asked him to lay out the linens, and I come back in from the kitchen a few minutes later, and the tablecloth on the main serving table is just... it's completely crooked. Hanging way off one side, all bunched up on the other. 

And I just... I don't know what came over me, but I completely blew up at him. I mean, I really let him have it. I was shouting, making a whole scene right there in the middle of the hall over a piece of fabric. And the thing is, I don't usually talk to people that way. I never have. I've always been a pretty level-headed guy, you can ask anyone who knows me. But that incident... it just really shook me up, realizing I'd lost my temper and yelled at a kid like that over something so trivial. My daughter told me I should probably come talk to someone about it, because it's just not like me at all to be snapping at folks.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think on that... no, not really. And I suppose that's what makes the whole tablecloth business so baffling to me, to be perfectly honest with you. I'm a creature of habit, you see. Have been since I retired from the plant back in '08. I get up at 6:15 every morning—don't even need an alarm clock anymore, my body just knows—I brew a pot of coffee, I read the sports section, and I go about my day. 

My mood is... well, it's just my mood. Steady. Always has been. My late wife, God rest her soul, used to say I was like an old grandfather clock, just ticking along, never too fast, never too slow. So no, I haven't been feeling down in the dumps, or jittery, or anything out of the ordinary. I still enjoy my morning crossword puzzles, I still watch my programs every evening at 7:00 sharp. 

My daughter, Sarah—she's the one who suggested I come see you, like I mentioned—she asked me the exact same thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Memory? Well, no, no... my memory is sharp as a tack, doctor. I mean, knock on wood, right? You get to be my age—I'll be eighty next year, you know—and you start seeing a lot of folks in your circle losing their keys, forgetting their grandkids' names, that sort of sad business. My buddy Frank, for instance, we worked on the line at the plant together for thirty years, and these days he can't remember what he had for breakfast half the time. But me? No, I'm clear as a bell. 

I still do the Sunday crossword in pen. The big one, mind you, not the easy little puzzle they put in the daily paper. I sit down with my coffee—just black, no sugar, my regular doctor says I have to watch my numbers—and I can focus on that puzzle for a good hour or two without my mind wandering one bit. So my concentration is just fine. Better than fine, really. Like I was saying about the community center, I notice the details. I noticed that tablecloth being crooked from all the way across the room, didn't I? I can focus on a task until it's done exactly right. 

If anything, I might be too focused on getting things the way they ought to be. But no, no fogginess, I'm not leaving the stove on or forgetting my appointments. My daughter Sarah even jokes that I have a better memory than she does, and she's only forty-two. Just last week I had to remind her that her car registration was due for renewal. She'd completely forgotten about it, but I remembered because she bought that little silver Honda the exact same month my oldest grandson started middle school, which was... well, anyway, the point is, my mind is working just as well as it always has. No issues up here at all.

No, no, nothing like that. Thank heavens. Like I said, I'm pretty sturdy for a fellow my age. I haven't had a fall in... gosh, I can't even tell you how long. I don't get dizzy spells, I don't lose my balance. I still drive myself to the grocery store, to the community center, everywhere I need to go. 

So it's certainly not a physical thing, not a bump on the head or anything of that nature. My brain is physically sound, doctor. That's why that whole business with the yelling was so out of left field. If I'd hit my head, well, maybe I could understand it, but no... physically, I'm right as rain.

I asked him to lay out the linens, and I come back in from the kitchen a few minutes later, and the tablecloth on the main serving table is just... it's completely crooked. Hanging way off one side, all bunched up on the other. \n\nAnd I just... I don't know what came over me, but I completely blew up at him. I mean, I really let him have it. I was shouting, making a whole scene right there in the middle of the hall over a piece of fabric. And the thing is, I don't usually talk to people that way. I never have. I've always been a pretty level-headed guy, you can ask anyone who knows me. But that incident... it just really shook me up, realizing I'd lost my temper and yelled at a kid like that over something so trivial. My daughter told me I should probably come talk to someone about it, because it's just not like me at all to be snapping at folks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your mood, behavior, or thinking lately, aside from this incident at the community center?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that... no, not really. And I suppose that's what makes the whole tablecloth business so baffling to me, to be perfectly honest with you. I'm a creature of habit, you see. Have been since I retired from the plant back in '08. I get up at 6:15 every morning—don't even need an alarm clock anymore, my body just knows—I brew a pot of coffee, I read the sports section, and I go about my day. \n\nMy mood is... well, it's just my mood. Steady. Always has been. My late wife, God rest her soul, used to say I was like an old grandfather clock, just ticking along, never too fast, never too slow. So no, I haven't been feeling down in the dumps, or jittery, or anything out of the ordinary. I still enjoy my morning crossword puzzles, I still watch my programs every evening at 7:00 sharp. \n\nMy daughter, Sarah—she's the one who suggested I come see you, like I mentioned—she asked me the exact same thing. She sat me down at my kitchen table on Tuesday, poured me a cup of tea, and said, \"Dad, are you feeling alright in yourself?\" And I told her exactly what I'm telling you. I feel like myself. I haven't been snapping at the cashier at the grocery store, I haven't been tossing and turning in my bed, nothing like that, if that's what you mean. It's really just that one flare-up that stands out. Just that one afternoon at the center where I lost my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory, concentration, or ability to think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? Well, no, no... my memory is sharp as a tack, doctor. I mean, knock on wood, right? You get to be my age—I'll be eighty next year, you know—and you start seeing a lot of folks in your circle losing their keys, forgetting their grandkids' names, that sort of sad business. My buddy Frank, for instance, we worked on the line at the plant together for thirty years, and these days he can't remember what he had for breakfast half the time. But me? No, I'm clear as a bell. \n\nI still do the Sunday crossword in pen. The big one, mind you, not the easy little puzzle they put in the daily paper. I sit down with my coffee—just black, no sugar, my regular doctor says I have to watch my numbers—and I can focus on that puzzle for a good hour or two without my mind wandering one bit. So my concentration is just fine. Better than fine, really. Like I was saying about the community center, I notice the details. I noticed that tablecloth being crooked from all the way across the room, didn't I? I can focus on a task until it's done exactly right. \n\nIf anything, I might be too focused on getting things the way they ought to be. But no, no fogginess, I'm not leaving the stove on or forgetting my appointments. My daughter Sarah even jokes that I have a better memory than she does, and she's only forty-two. Just last week I had to remind her that her car registration was due for renewal. She'd completely forgotten about it, but I remembered because she bought that little silver Honda the exact same month my oldest grandson started middle school, which was... well, anyway, the point is, my mind is working just as well as it always has. No issues up here at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injuries, falls, or any physical health problems that might have affected your brain function, like a stroke or mini-strokes?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. Thank heavens. Like I said, I'm pretty sturdy for a fellow my age. I haven't had a fall in... gosh, I can't even tell you how long. I'm very careful about where I step, you know. When you get to be my age, a fall is nothing to sneeze at. I've seen too many guys go down, break a hip, and then it's just downhill from there. So I wear sensible shoes, good grip on the soles. Bought a pair of those orthopedic walking shoes last spring, actually, from that catalog that comes in the mail. The brown leather ones with the velcro straps. Very supportive. \n\nAnd as for strokes or mini-strokes, no, my regular doctor, Dr. Henderson—been seeing him for twenty years now—he keeps a close eye on all that. My blood pressure is good, my cholesterol is right where it should be, considering I still enjoy a piece of bacon on Sunday mornings. Everything is ticking along just fine physically. I don't get dizzy spells, I don't lose my balance. I still drive myself to the grocery store, to the community center, everywhere I need to go. \n\nSo it's certainly not a physical thing, not a bump on the head or anything of that nature. My brain is physically sound, doctor. That's why that whole business with the yelling was so out of left field. If I'd hit my head, well, maybe I could understand it, but no... physically, I'm right as rain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If I'd hit my head, well, maybe I could understand it, but no... physically, I'm right as rain.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever used any recreational drugs or substances, either recently or in the past, such as marijuana, alcohol, or anything else that might affect your mood or behavior?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 79 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Drugs? Oh, Lord, no. Never touched the stuff. I mean, you hear about these things on the news, all these young folks getting mixed up in whatever new pill or powder is out there, but no, that's never been my world. Even back in the sixties and seventies, when all that was going on... well, I was too busy working at the plant, providing for my wife and my little girl, to have any time for nonsense like that. I've always been a straight shooter, you see. Never saw the appeal of losing control of my own mind. 

Now, as for alcohol, well, I'm a moderate man, doctor. Always have been. I might have a small glass of beer if I'm at a summer barbecue—usually a domestic, none of that fancy craft stuff they sell nowadays that tastes like pine needles—or maybe a little sip of champagne at a wedding toast, if it's a family affair. But drinking to get drunk? No, sir. Never been my style. My father, rest his soul, he liked his whiskey a bit too much, and I saw early on what that does to a man, how it changes him. Made a promise to myself a long, long time ago that I'd never go down that road. 

So no, no drugs, no heavy drinking, nothing of the sort. Just my morning coffee and maybe a cup of decaf tea in the evening while I watch my programs. I've always believed that if you treat your body right, keep your wits about you, you'll be just fine. That's why I'm sitting here, really, trying to figure out what happened at the center, because it certainly wasn't brought on by anything I put in my body. Clean as a whistle, that's me. Always have been.

My buddy Frank, for instance, we worked on the line at the plant together for thirty years, and these days he can't remember what he had for breakfast half the time. But me? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Changes in my personality? No, no, I wouldn't say so. And nobody's said a word to me about anything like that. I mean, my daughter Sarah—the one with the Honda I mentioned—she calls me every Sunday evening, regular as clockwork. Seven o'clock, right after she finishes her dinner dishes. We talk for a good half hour, sometimes forty-five minutes if the grandkids are acting up and she needs to vent. And she's never once said, "Dad, you're acting strange," or "Dad, you're not yourself." She's a sharp girl, Sarah is. Works in human resources over at the hospital, so she deals with people all day long. If I was acting peculiar, she'd be the first one to spot it and she wouldn't hold back, either. She's got her mother's temperament that way—very direct, very... observant.

And then there's Frank, my buddy from the plant. We still meet up for coffee every Tuesday morning at the diner on Main Street. We sit in the same booth, corner booth by the window, and we talk about the old days, complain about the local sports teams—the baseball team this year, honestly, they couldn't hit water if they fell out of a boat. But Frank, even with his memory slipping a bit, he knows me better than anyone since my wife passed. He hasn't looked at me funny or asked if I'm feeling alright in the head. We just have our coffee, split a piece of cherry pie, and go our separate ways. 

Even the folks at the community center, before... well, before that incident with the yelling... nobody treated me any differently. I was just the same old guy who comes in to play cards on Thursdays and helps set up the chairs for the bingo on Fridays. I've always been a bit particular about how things are done, sure. Like I said about the tablecloths, I like things neat and orderly. But that's not a change, doctor, that's just who I am. Have been for seventy-nine years. So no, nobody's come to me with any concerns about my personality. I'm just me. Same as always.

I can focus on a task until it's done exactly right. \n\nIf anything, I might be too focused on getting things the way they ought to be. But no, no fogginess, I'm not leaving the stove on or forgetting my appointments. My daughter Sarah even jokes that I have a better memory than she does, and she's only forty-two. Just last week I had to remind her that her car registration was due for renewal. She'd completely forgotten about it, but I remembered because she bought that little silver Honda the exact same month my oldest grandson started middle school, which was... well, anyway, the point is, my mind is working just as well as it always has. No issues up here at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injuries, falls, or any physical health problems that might have affected your brain function, like a stroke or mini-strokes?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. Thank heavens. Like I said, I'm pretty sturdy for a fellow my age. I haven't had a fall in... gosh, I can't even tell you how long. I'm very careful about where I step, you know. When you get to be my age, a fall is nothing to sneeze at. I've seen too many guys go down, break a hip, and then it's just downhill from there. So I wear sensible shoes, good grip on the soles. Bought a pair of those orthopedic walking shoes last spring, actually, from that catalog that comes in the mail. The brown leather ones with the velcro straps. Very supportive. \n\nAnd as for strokes or mini-strokes, no, my regular doctor, Dr. Henderson—been seeing him for twenty years now—he keeps a close eye on all that. My blood pressure is good, my cholesterol is right where it should be, considering I still enjoy a piece of bacon on Sunday mornings. Everything is ticking along just fine physically. I don't get dizzy spells, I don't lose my balance. I still drive myself to the grocery store, to the community center, everywhere I need to go. \n\nSo it's certainly not a physical thing, not a bump on the head or anything of that nature. My brain is physically sound, doctor. That's why that whole business with the yelling was so out of left field. If I'd hit my head, well, maybe I could understand it, but no... physically, I'm right as rain.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used any recreational drugs or substances, either recently or in the past, such as marijuana, alcohol, or anything else that might affect your mood or behavior?\n\nPatient: Drugs? But drinking to get drunk? No, sir. Never been my style. My father, rest his soul, he liked his whiskey a bit too much, and I saw early on what that does to a man, how it changes him. That's why I'm sitting here, really, trying to figure out what happened at the center, because it certainly wasn't brought on by anything I put in my body. Clean as a whistle, that's me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now, see... that's the thing. That's exactly why I'm sitting here in this chair right now, doctor. Because, normally, no. I'm a patient man. I raised a daughter, I worked thirty-five years on a factory floor with all sorts of characters, you have to be patient. But lately... well, specifically at the community center the other day, I just... I don't know what came over me. 

I was there early, like I always am on Thursdays for the card games, and they had these young volunteers setting up. High school kids, probably needing hours for some civics class or something. And this one boy, nice enough looking kid, but he was just throwing the tablecloths on the tables. Not smoothing them out, not lining up the edges with the corners of the tables. Just tossing them down like they were old rags. And they were crooked. So crooked. 

Normally, I'd just walk over, maybe make a little joke about how geometry wasn't his strong suit, and fix it myself. I've done it a hundred times. But this time... I just felt this heat rise up in my chest, right up into my neck. And before I even knew what I was doing, I was yelling at him. I mean, really raising my voice, telling him he was careless and disrespectful and if he couldn't do a simple job right he shouldn't be there at all. 

The poor kid looked terrified, just froze right there with a plastic fork in his hand. And the whole room went quiet. You could have heard a pin drop on that linoleum floor. Mrs. Higgins, who runs the desk, she came hurrying over looking at me like I'd grown two heads. And I... I just didn't know what to say. I don't talk to people that way. I've never talked to people that way, not even when the guys at the plant would mess up a run on the line. It was so out of character, so... ugly. I felt awful about it, still do. I apologized, of course, but the damage was done. That's what really scared me, doctor. That quick temper, just snapping over a crooked piece of plastic. It just wasn't me.

I haven't been snapping at the cashier at the grocery store, I haven't been tossing and turning in my bed, nothing like that, if that's what you mean. It's really just that one flare-up that stands out. Just that one afternoon at the center where I lost my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory, concentration, or ability to think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? Well, no, no... my memory is sharp as a tack, doctor. I mean, knock on wood, right? You get to be my age—I'll be eighty next year, you know—and you start seeing a lot of folks in your circle losing their keys, forgetting their grandkids' names, that sort of sad business. My buddy Frank, for instance, we worked on the line at the plant together for thirty years, and these days he can't remember what he had for breakfast half the time. But me? No, I'm clear as a bell. \n\nI still do the Sunday crossword in pen. The big one, mind you, not the easy little puzzle they put in the daily paper. I sit down with my coffee—just black, no sugar, my regular doctor says I have to watch my numbers—and I can focus on that puzzle for a good hour or two without my mind wandering one bit. So my concentration is just fine. Better than fine, really. Like I was saying about the community center, I notice the details. I noticed that tablecloth being crooked from all the way across the room, didn't I? I can focus on a task until it's done exactly right. \n\nIf anything, I might be too focused on getting things the way they ought to be. But no, no fogginess, I'm not leaving the stove on or forgetting my appointments. My daughter Sarah even jokes that I have a better memory than she does, and she's only forty-two. Just last week I had to remind her that her car registration was due for renewal. She'd completely forgotten about it, but I remembered because she bought that little silver Honda the exact same month my oldest grandson started middle school, which was... well, anyway, the point is, my mind is working just as well as it always has. No issues up here at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injuries, falls, or any physical health problems that might have affected your brain function, like a stroke or mini-strokes?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. Thank heavens. Like I said, I'm pretty sturdy for a fellow my age. I haven't had a fall in... gosh, I can't even tell you how long. I'm very careful about where I step, you know. When you get to be my age, a fall is nothing to sneeze at. I've seen too many guys go down, break a hip, and then it's just downhill from there. So I wear sensible shoes, good grip on the soles. Bought a pair of those orthopedic walking shoes last spring, actually, from that catalog that comes in the mail. The brown leather ones with the velcro straps. Very supportive. \n\nAnd as for strokes or mini-strokes, no, my regular doctor, Dr. Henderson—been seeing him for twenty years now—he keeps a close eye on all that. My blood pressure is good, my cholesterol is right where it should be, considering I still enjoy a piece of bacon on Sunday mornings. Everything is ticking along just fine physically. I don't get dizzy spells, I don't lose my balance. I still drive myself to the grocery store, to the community center, everywhere I need to go. \n\nSo it's certainly not a physical thing, not a bump on the head or anything of that nature. My brain is physically sound, doctor. That's why that whole business with the yelling was so out of left field. If I'd hit my head, well, maybe I could understand it, but no... physically, I'm right as rain.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used any recreational drugs or substances, either recently or in the past, such as marijuana, alcohol, or anything else that might affect your mood or behavior?\n\nPatient: Drugs? Oh, Lord, no. Never touched the stuff. I mean, you hear about these things on the news, all these young folks getting mixed up in whatever new pill or powder is out there, but no, that's never been my world. Even back in the sixties and seventies, when all that was going on... well, I was too busy working at the plant, providing for my wife and my little girl, to have any time for nonsense like that. I've always been a straight shooter, you see. Never saw the appeal of losing control of my own mind. \n\nNow, as for alcohol, well, I'm a moderate man, doctor. Always have been. I might have a small glass of beer if I'm at a summer barbecue—usually a domestic, none of that fancy craft stuff they sell nowadays that tastes like pine needles—or maybe a little sip of champagne at a wedding toast, if it's a family affair. But drinking to get drunk? No, sir. Never been my style. My father, rest his soul, he liked his whiskey a bit too much, and I saw early on what that does to a man, how it changes him. Made a promise to myself a long, long time ago that I'd never go down that road. \n\nSo no, no drugs, no heavy drinking, nothing of the sort. Just my morning coffee and maybe a cup of decaf tea in the evening while I watch my programs. I've always believed that if you treat your body right, keep your wits about you, you'll be just fine. That's why I'm sitting here, really, trying to figure out what happened at the center, because it certainly wasn't brought on by anything I put in my body. Clean as a whistle, that's me. Always have been.\n\nDoctor: Have any family members or close friends noticed any changes in your personality or behavior that you might not be aware of?\n\nPatient: Changes in my personality? No, no, I wouldn't say so. And nobody's said a word to me about anything like that. I mean, my daughter Sarah—the one with the Honda I mentioned—she calls me every Sunday evening, regular as clockwork. Seven o'clock, right after she finishes her dinner dishes. We talk for a good half hour, sometimes forty-five minutes if the grandkids are acting up and she needs to vent. And she's never once said, \"Dad, you're acting strange,\" or \"Dad, you're not yourself.\" She's a sharp girl, Sarah is. Works in human resources over at the hospital, so she deals with people all day long. If I was acting peculiar, she'd be the first one to spot it and she wouldn't hold back, either. She's got her mother's temperament that way—very direct, very... observant.\n\nAnd then there's Frank, my buddy from the plant. We still meet up for coffee every Tuesday morning at the diner on Main Street. We sit in the same booth, corner booth by the window, and we talk about the old days, complain about the local sports teams—the baseball team this year, honestly, they couldn't hit water if they fell out of a boat. But Frank, even with his memory slipping a bit, he knows me better than anyone since my wife passed. He hasn't looked at me funny or asked if I'm feeling alright in the head. We just have our coffee, split a piece of cherry pie, and go our separate ways. \n\nEven the folks at the community center, before... well, before that incident with the yelling... nobody treated me any differently. I was just the same old guy who comes in to play cards on Thursdays and helps set up the chairs for the bingo on Fridays. I've always been a bit particular about how things are done, sure. Like I said about the tablecloths, I like things neat and orderly. But that's not a change, doctor, that's just who I am. Have been for seventy-nine years. So no, nobody's come to me with any concerns about my personality. I'm just me. Same as always.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself becoming more easily frustrated or impatient, even in small things, that you might not have noticed before?\n\nPatient: Well, now, see... that's the thing. That's exactly why I'm sitting here in this chair right now, doctor. Because, normally, no. I'm a patient man. I raised a daughter, I worked thirty-five years on a factory floor with all sorts of characters, you have to be patient. But lately... well, specifically at the community center the other day, I just... I don't know what came over me. \n\nI was there early, like I always am on Thursdays for the card games, and they had these young volunteers setting up. High school kids, probably needing hours for some civics class or something. And this one boy, nice enough looking kid, but he was just throwing the tablecloths on the tables. Not smoothing them out, not lining up the edges with the corners of the tables. Just tossing them down like they were old rags. And they were crooked. So crooked. \n\nNormally, I'd just walk over, maybe make a little joke about how geometry wasn't his strong suit, and fix it myself. I've done it a hundred times. But this time... I just felt this heat rise up in my chest, right up into my neck. And before I even knew what I was doing, I was yelling at him. I mean, really raising my voice, telling him he was careless and disrespectful and if he couldn't do a simple job right he shouldn't be there at all. \n\nThe poor kid looked terrified, just froze right there with a plastic fork in his hand. And the whole room went quiet. You could have heard a pin drop on that linoleum floor. Mrs. Higgins, who runs the desk, she came hurrying over looking at me like I'd grown two heads. And I... I just didn't know what to say. I don't talk to people that way. I've never talked to people that way, not even when the guys at the plant would mess up a run on the line. It was so out of character, so... ugly. I felt awful about it, still do. I apologized, of course, but the damage was done. That's what really scared me, doctor. That quick temper, just snapping over a crooked piece of plastic. It just wasn't me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think about that. Since Thursday... Thursday afternoon I just went straight home. I didn't even stay for the rest of the setup or the card game, I was so rattled by my own behavior. I got in the Buick, drove back to the house—hit every single red light on Elm Street, which usually gets a little sigh out of me, but I was just so focused on what I'd done. 

Have I had another rush of anger? Well, not a full blow-up, no. I haven't yelled at anyone else. I did get a little... a little hot under the collar yesterday morning when the paperboy tossed the Sunday edition right into the middle of my wife's old rosebushes instead of on the porch where it belongs. I felt my jaw clench up real tight, and my heart started beating a little faster, and I muttered a few choice words under my breath while I was out there fishing it out of the thorns. But I didn't yell. I didn't go chasing down the street after the boy on his bicycle. 

Because, honestly, doctor, that one time at the center... that was more than enough to scare me straight into this office. I mean, like I was trying to explain, what brought me in is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I don't usually talk to people that way. I've been turning it over and over in my head ever since. Why did I do it? Why a tablecloth? It’s just a cheap piece of plastic from the party store down the road. And the look on that poor kid's face... I keep seeing it every time I sit down in my armchair to watch the evening news. 

So no, no other big outbursts, thank goodness, because I don't think my conscience could take it. But that one time was enough to let me know something isn't sitting right. Seventy-nine years of keeping my cool on the factory floor, and then... boom. Over a tablecloth. It's just not right, doctor, it really isn't. I just can't seem to let it go.

Just last week I had to remind her that her car registration was due for renewal. No issues up here at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injuries, falls, or any physical health problems that might have affected your brain function, like a stroke or mini-strokes?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. Bought a pair of those orthopedic walking shoes last spring, actually, from that catalog that comes in the mail. The brown leather ones with the velcro straps. Very supportive. \n\nAnd as for strokes or mini-strokes, no, my regular doctor, Dr. Henderson—been seeing him for twenty years now—he keeps a close eye on all that. My blood pressure is good, my cholesterol is right where it should be, considering I still enjoy a piece of bacon on Sunday mornings. Everything is ticking along just fine physically. I don't get dizzy spells, I don't lose my balance. I still drive myself to the grocery store, to the community center, everywhere I need to go. \n\nSo it's certainly not a physical thing, not a bump on the head or anything of that nature. My brain is physically sound, doctor. That's why that whole business with the yelling was so out of left field. Oh, Lord, no. Never touched the stuff. I mean, you hear about these things on the news, all these young folks getting mixed up in whatever new pill or powder is out there, but no, that's never been my world. Even back in the sixties and seventies, when all that was going on... well, I was too busy working at the plant, providing for my wife and my little girl, to have any time for nonsense like that. I've always been a straight shooter, you see. Never saw the appeal of losing control of my own mind. \n\nNow, as for alcohol, well, I'm a moderate man, doctor. Always have been. Made a promise to myself a long, long time ago that I'd never go down that road. \n\nSo no, no drugs, no heavy drinking, nothing of the sort. Just my morning coffee and maybe a cup of decaf tea in the evening while I watch my programs. I've always believed that if you treat your body right, keep your wits about you, you'll be just fine. That's why I'm sitting here, really, trying to figure out what happened at the center, because it certainly wasn't brought on by anything I put in my body. Clean as a whistle, that's me. Always have been.\n\nDoctor: Have any family members or close friends noticed any changes in your personality or behavior that you might not be aware of?\n\nPatient: Changes in my personality? No, no, I wouldn't say so. And nobody's said a word to me about anything like that. I mean, my daughter Sarah—the one with the Honda I mentioned—she calls me every Sunday evening, regular as clockwork. Seven o'clock, right after she finishes her dinner dishes. We talk for a good half hour, sometimes forty-five minutes if the grandkids are acting up and she needs to vent. And she's never once said, \"Dad, you're acting strange,\" or \"Dad, you're not yourself.\" She's a sharp girl, Sarah is. Works in human resources over at the hospital, so she deals with people all day long. If I was acting peculiar, she'd be the first one to spot it and she wouldn't hold back, either. She's got her mother's temperament that way—very direct, very... observant.\n\nAnd then there's Frank, my buddy from the plant. We still meet up for coffee every Tuesday morning at the diner on Main Street. We sit in the same booth, corner booth by the window, and we talk about the old days, complain about the local sports teams—the baseball team this year, honestly, they couldn't hit water if they fell out of a boat. But Frank, even with his memory slipping a bit, he knows me better than anyone since my wife passed. He hasn't looked at me funny or asked if I'm feeling alright in the head. We just have our coffee, split a piece of cherry pie, and go our separate ways. \n\nEven the folks at the community center, before... well, before that incident with the yelling... nobody treated me any differently. I was just the same old guy who comes in to play cards on Thursdays and helps set up the chairs for the bingo on Fridays. I've always been a bit particular about how things are done, sure. Like I said about the tablecloths, I like things neat and orderly. But that's not a change, doctor, that's just who I am. Have been for seventy-nine years. So no, nobody's come to me with any concerns about my personality. I'm just me. Same as always.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself becoming more easily frustrated or impatient, even in small things, that you might not have noticed before?\n\nPatient: Well, now, see... that's the thing. That's exactly why I'm sitting here in this chair right now, doctor. Because, normally, no. I'm a patient man. I raised a daughter, I worked thirty-five years on a factory floor with all sorts of characters, you have to be patient. But lately... well, specifically at the community center the other day, I just... I don't know what came over me. \n\nI was there early, like I always am on Thursdays for the card games, and they had these young volunteers setting up. High school kids, probably needing hours for some civics class or something. And this one boy, nice enough looking kid, but he was just throwing the tablecloths on the tables. Not smoothing them out, not lining up the edges with the corners of the tables. Just tossing them down like they were old rags. And they were crooked. So crooked. \n\nNormally, I'd just walk over, maybe make a little joke about how geometry wasn't his strong suit, and fix it myself. I've done it a hundred times. But this time... I just felt this heat rise up in my chest, right up into my neck. And before I even knew what I was doing, I was yelling at him. I mean, really raising my voice, telling him he was careless and disrespectful and if he couldn't do a simple job right he shouldn't be there at all. \n\nThe poor kid looked terrified, just froze right there with a plastic fork in his hand. And the whole room went quiet. You could have heard a pin drop on that linoleum floor. Mrs. Higgins, who runs the desk, she came hurrying over looking at me like I'd grown two heads. And I... I just didn't know what to say. I don't talk to people that way. I've never talked to people that way, not even when the guys at the plant would mess up a run on the line. It was so out of character, so... ugly. I felt awful about it, still do. I apologized, of course, but the damage was done. That's what really scared me, doctor. That quick temper, just snapping over a crooked piece of plastic. It just wasn't me.\n\nDoctor: Since that incident at the community center, have you had any other moments where you felt that sudden rush of anger or frustration over something small?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that. Since Thursday... Thursday afternoon I just went straight home. I didn't even stay for the rest of the setup or the card game, I was so rattled by my own behavior. I got in the Buick, drove back to the house—hit every single red light on Elm Street, which usually gets a little sigh out of me, but I was just so focused on what I'd done. \n\nHave I had another rush of anger? Well, not a full blow-up, no. I haven't yelled at anyone else. I did get a little... a little hot under the collar yesterday morning when the paperboy tossed the Sunday edition right into the middle of my wife's old rosebushes instead of on the porch where it belongs. I felt my jaw clench up real tight, and my heart started beating a little faster, and I muttered a few choice words under my breath while I was out there fishing it out of the thorns. But I didn't yell. I didn't go chasing down the street after the boy on his bicycle. \n\nBecause, honestly, doctor, that one time at the center... that was more than enough to scare me straight into this office. I mean, like I was trying to explain, what brought me in is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I don't usually talk to people that way. I've been turning it over and over in my head ever since. Why did I do it? Why a tablecloth? It’s just a cheap piece of plastic from the party store down the road. And the look on that poor kid's face... I keep seeing it every time I sit down in my armchair to watch the evening news. \n\nSo no, no other big outbursts, thank goodness, because I don't think my conscience could take it. But that one time was enough to let me know something isn't sitting right. Seventy-nine years of keeping my cool on the factory floor, and then... boom. Over a tablecloth. It just wasn't me.\n\nDoctor: Since that incident at the community center, have you had any other moments where you felt that sudden rush of anger or frustration over something small?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that. Since Thursday... Thursday afternoon I just went straight home. I didn't even stay for the rest of the setup or the card game, I was so rattled by my own behavior. I got in the Buick, drove back to the house—hit every single red light on Elm Street, which usually gets a little sigh out of me, but I was just so focused on what I'd done. \n\nHave I had another rush of anger? Well, not a full blow-up, no. I haven't yelled at anyone else. I did get a little... a little hot under the collar yesterday morning when the paperboy tossed the Sunday edition right into the middle of my wife's old rosebushes instead of on the porch where it belongs. I felt my jaw clench up real tight, and my heart started beating a little faster, and I muttered a few choice words under my breath while I was out there fishing it out of the thorns. But I didn't yell. I didn't go chasing down the street after the boy on his bicycle. \n\nBecause, honestly, doctor, that one time at the center... that was more than enough to scare me straight into this office. I mean, like I was trying to explain, what brought me in is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I don't usually talk to people that way. I've been turning it over and over in my head ever since. Why did I do it? Why a tablecloth? It’s just a cheap piece of plastic from the party store down the road. And the look on that poor kid's face... I keep seeing it every time I sit down in my armchair to watch the evening news. \n\nSo no, no other big outbursts, thank goodness, because I don't think my conscience could take it. But that one time was enough to let me know something isn't sitting right. Seventy-nine years of keeping my cool on the factory floor, and then... boom. Over a tablecloth. It's just not right, doctor, it really isn't. I just can't seem to let it go.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas there been anything else recently, maybe even outside the community center, where you felt unusually frustrated or upset over small things being out of place or not done right?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 79 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Irritability_Aggression\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I don't usually talk to people that way.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, outside the center... I mean, I mentioned the paperboy with the rosebushes, but... honestly, doctor, it really all comes back to that one moment. I know we keep circling back to it, but it's just weighing on me so heavily. Because, like I said, what brought me in here, sitting in this very office, is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I just don't usually talk to people that way. 

I've been trying to think if there was anything else, like maybe at the grocery store? Sometimes the baggers put the canned goods right on top of the bread, and that used to just make me shake my head a bit, but I haven't yelled at anyone there. I haven't snapped at the tellers at the bank, even when they take forever with the deposit slips. It really was just that one awful, embarrassing outburst at the community center. 

I keep replaying it, you know? The way the plastic was hanging off the edge of the table, the way the kid was just tossing them down without a care in the world... and then the sound of my own voice echoing off those cinderblock walls. It was so loud. I didn't even know I had that kind of volume left in me at my age. And the look on his face... oh, it just makes my stomach tie up in knots just thinking about it. I've always prided myself on being a gentleman, on keeping a level head. Thirty-five years at the plant, you learn to let the little things slide, or you'd drive yourself crazy. But that day, over a tablecloth... I just lost it. And that's why I'm here. Because that's not me. It's just not me at all.

When you get to be my age, a fall is nothing to sneeze at. I've seen too many guys go down, break a hip, and then it's just downhill from there. So I wear sensible shoes, good grip on the soles. Bought a pair of those orthopedic walking shoes last spring, actually, from that catalog that comes in the mail. The brown leather ones with the velcro straps. Very supportive. \n\nAnd as for strokes or mini-strokes, no, my regular doctor, Dr. Henderson—been seeing him for twenty years now—he keeps a close eye on all that. My blood pressure is good, my cholesterol is right where it should be, considering I still enjoy a piece of bacon on Sunday mornings. Everything is ticking along just fine physically. I don't get dizzy spells, I don't lose my balance. I still drive myself to the grocery store, to the community center, everywhere I need to go. \n\nSo it's certainly not a physical thing, not a bump on the head or anything of that nature. My brain is physically sound, doctor. That's why that whole business with the yelling was so out of left field. If I'd hit my head, well, maybe I could understand it, but no... physically, I'm right as rain.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used any recreational drugs or substances, either recently or in the past, such as marijuana, alcohol, or anything else that might affect your mood or behavior?\n\nPatient: Drugs? Oh, Lord, no. Never touched the stuff. I mean, you hear about these things on the news, all these young folks getting mixed up in whatever new pill or powder is out there, but no, that's never been my world. Even back in the sixties and seventies, when all that was going on... well, I was too busy working at the plant, providing for my wife and my little girl, to have any time for nonsense like that. I've always been a straight shooter, you see. Never saw the appeal of losing control of my own mind. \n\nNow, as for alcohol, well, I'm a moderate man, doctor. Always have been. I might have a small glass of beer if I'm at a summer barbecue—usually a domestic, none of that fancy craft stuff they sell nowadays that tastes like pine needles—or maybe a little sip of champagne at a wedding toast, if it's a family affair. But drinking to get drunk? No, sir. Never been my style. My father, rest his soul, he liked his whiskey a bit too much, and I saw early on what that does to a man, how it changes him. Made a promise to myself a long, long time ago that I'd never go down that road. \n\nSo no, no drugs, no heavy drinking, nothing of the sort. Just my morning coffee and maybe a cup of decaf tea in the evening while I watch my programs. I've always believed that if you treat your body right, keep your wits about you, you'll be just fine. That's why I'm sitting here, really, trying to figure out what happened at the center, because it certainly wasn't brought on by anything I put in my body. Clean as a whistle, that's me. Always have been.\n\nDoctor: Have any family members or close friends noticed any changes in your personality or behavior that you might not be aware of?\n\nPatient: Changes in my personality? No, no, I wouldn't say so. And nobody's said a word to me about anything like that. We talk for a good half hour, sometimes forty-five minutes if the grandkids are acting up and she needs to vent. And she's never once said, \"Dad, you're acting strange,\" or \"Dad, you're not yourself.\" She's a sharp girl, Sarah is. Works in human resources over at the hospital, so she deals with people all day long. If I was acting peculiar, she'd be the first one to spot it and she wouldn't hold back, either. She's got her mother's temperament that way—very direct, very... observant.\n\nAnd then there's Frank, my buddy from the plant. We still meet up for coffee every Tuesday morning at the diner on Main Street. We sit in the same booth, corner booth by the window, and we talk about the old days, complain about the local sports teams—the baseball team this year, honestly, they couldn't hit water if they fell out of a boat. But Frank, even with his memory slipping a bit, he knows me better than anyone since my wife passed. He hasn't looked at me funny or asked if I'm feeling alright in the head. We just have our coffee, split a piece of cherry pie, and go our separate ways. \n\nEven the folks at the community center, before... well, before that incident with the yelling... nobody treated me any differently. I was just the same old guy who comes in to play cards on Thursdays and helps set up the chairs for the bingo on Fridays. I've always been a bit particular about how things are done, sure. Like I said about the tablecloths, I like things neat and orderly. But that's not a change, doctor, that's just who I am. Have been for seventy-nine years. So no, nobody's come to me with any concerns about my personality. I'm just me. Not smoothing them out, not lining up the edges with the corners of the tables. Just tossing them down like they were old rags. And they were crooked. So crooked. \n\nNormally, I'd just walk over, maybe make a little joke about how geometry wasn't his strong suit, and fix it myself. I've done it a hundred times. But this time... I just didn't know what to say. I don't talk to people that way. I've never talked to people that way, not even when the guys at the plant would mess up a run on the line. It was so out of character, so... ugly. I felt awful about it, still do. I apologized, of course, but the damage was done. That's what really scared me, doctor. It just wasn't me.\n\nDoctor: Since that incident at the community center, have you had any other moments where you felt that sudden rush of anger or frustration over something small?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that. If I'd hit my head, well, maybe I could understand it, but no... physically, I'm right as rain.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used any recreational drugs or substances, either recently or in the past, such as marijuana, alcohol, or anything else that might affect your mood or behavior?\n\nPatient: Drugs? Oh, Lord, no. Never touched the stuff. I mean, you hear about these things on the news, all these young folks getting mixed up in whatever new pill or powder is out there, but no, that's never been my world. Even back in the sixties and seventies, when all that was going on... well, I was too busy working at the plant, providing for my wife and my little girl, to have any time for nonsense like that. I've always been a straight shooter, you see. Never saw the appeal of losing control of my own mind. \n\nNow, as for alcohol, well, I'm a moderate man, doctor. Always have been. I might have a small glass of beer if I'm at a summer barbecue—usually a domestic, none of that fancy craft stuff they sell nowadays that tastes like pine needles—or maybe a little sip of champagne at a wedding toast, if it's a family affair. But drinking to get drunk? No, sir. Never been my style. My father, rest his soul, he liked his whiskey a bit too much, and I saw early on what that does to a man, how it changes him. Made a promise to myself a long, long time ago that I'd never go down that road. \n\nSo no, no drugs, no heavy drinking, nothing of the sort. Just my morning coffee and maybe a cup of decaf tea in the evening while I watch my programs. I've always believed that if you treat your body right, keep your wits about you, you'll be just fine. That's why I'm sitting here, really, trying to figure out what happened at the center, because it certainly wasn't brought on by anything I put in my body. Clean as a whistle, that's me. Always have been.\n\nDoctor: Have any family members or close friends noticed any changes in your personality or behavior that you might not be aware of?\n\nPatient: Changes in my personality? No, no, I wouldn't say so. And nobody's said a word to me about anything like that. I mean, my daughter Sarah—the one with the Honda I mentioned—she calls me every Sunday evening, regular as clockwork. Seven o'clock, right after she finishes her dinner dishes. We talk for a good half hour, sometimes forty-five minutes if the grandkids are acting up and she needs to vent. And she's never once said, \"Dad, you're acting strange,\" or \"Dad, you're not yourself.\" She's a sharp girl, Sarah is. Works in human resources over at the hospital, so she deals with people all day long. If I was acting peculiar, she'd be the first one to spot it and she wouldn't hold back, either. She's got her mother's temperament that way—very direct, very... observant.\n\nAnd then there's Frank, my buddy from the plant. We still meet up for coffee every Tuesday morning at the diner on Main Street. We sit in the same booth, corner booth by the window, and we talk about the old days, complain about the local sports teams—the baseball team this year, honestly, they couldn't hit water if they fell out of a boat. But Frank, even with his memory slipping a bit, he knows me better than anyone since my wife passed. He hasn't looked at me funny or asked if I'm feeling alright in the head. We just have our coffee, split a piece of cherry pie, and go our separate ways. \n\nEven the folks at the community center, before... well, before that incident with the yelling... nobody treated me any differently. I was just the same old guy who comes in to play cards on Thursdays and helps set up the chairs for the bingo on Fridays. I've always been a bit particular about how things are done, sure. Like I said about the tablecloths, I like things neat and orderly. But that's not a change, doctor, that's just who I am. Have been for seventy-nine years. So no, nobody's come to me with any concerns about my personality. I'm just me. Same as always.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself becoming more easily frustrated or impatient, even in small things, that you might not have noticed before?\n\nPatient: Well, now, see... that's the thing. That's exactly why I'm sitting here in this chair right now, doctor. Because, normally, no. I'm a patient man. I raised a daughter, I worked thirty-five years on a factory floor with all sorts of characters, you have to be patient. But lately... well, specifically at the community center the other day, I just... I don't know what came over me. \n\nI was there early, like I always am on Thursdays for the card games, and they had these young volunteers setting up. High school kids, probably needing hours for some civics class or something. And this one boy, nice enough looking kid, but he was just throwing the tablecloths on the tables. Not smoothing them out, not lining up the edges with the corners of the tables. Just tossing them down like they were old rags. And they were crooked. So crooked. \n\nNormally, I'd just walk over, maybe make a little joke about how geometry wasn't his strong suit, and fix it myself. I've done it a hundred times. But this time... I just felt this heat rise up in my chest, right up into my neck. And before I even knew what I was doing, I was yelling at him. I mean, really raising my voice, telling him he was careless and disrespectful and if he couldn't do a simple job right he shouldn't be there at all. \n\nThe poor kid looked terrified, just froze right there with a plastic fork in his hand. And the whole room went quiet. You could have heard a pin drop on that linoleum floor. Mrs. Higgins, who runs the desk, she came hurrying over looking at me like I'd grown two heads. And I... I just didn't know what to say. I don't talk to people that way. I've never talked to people that way, not even when the guys at the plant would mess up a run on the line. It was so out of character, so... ugly. I felt awful about it, still do. I apologized, of course, but the damage was done. That's what really scared me, doctor. That quick temper, just snapping over a crooked piece of plastic. It just wasn't me.\n\nDoctor: Since that incident at the community center, have you had any other moments where you felt that sudden rush of anger or frustration over something small?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that. Since Thursday... Thursday afternoon I just went straight home. I didn't even stay for the rest of the setup or the card game, I was so rattled by my own behavior. I got in the Buick, drove back to the house—hit every single red light on Elm Street, which usually gets a little sigh out of me, but I was just so focused on what I'd done. \n\nHave I had another rush of anger? Well, not a full blow-up, no. I haven't yelled at anyone else. I did get a little... a little hot under the collar yesterday morning when the paperboy tossed the Sunday edition right into the middle of my wife's old rosebushes instead of on the porch where it belongs. I felt my jaw clench up real tight, and my heart started beating a little faster, and I muttered a few choice words under my breath while I was out there fishing it out of the thorns. But I didn't yell. I didn't go chasing down the street after the boy on his bicycle. \n\nBecause, honestly, doctor, that one time at the center... that was more than enough to scare me straight into this office. I mean, like I was trying to explain, what brought me in is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I don't usually talk to people that way. I've been turning it over and over in my head ever since. Why did I do it? Why a tablecloth? It’s just a cheap piece of plastic from the party store down the road. And the look on that poor kid's face... I keep seeing it every time I sit down in my armchair to watch the evening news. \n\nSo no, no other big outbursts, thank goodness, because I don't think my conscience could take it. But that one time was enough to let me know something isn't sitting right. Seventy-nine years of keeping my cool on the factory floor, and then... boom. Over a tablecloth. It's just not right, doctor, it really isn't. I just can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Has there been anything else recently, maybe even outside the community center, where you felt unusually frustrated or upset over small things being out of place or not done right?\n\nPatient: Well, outside the center... I mean, I mentioned the paperboy with the rosebushes, but... honestly, doctor, it really all comes back to that one moment. I know we keep circling back to it, but it's just weighing on me so heavily. Because, like I said, what brought me in here, sitting in this very office, is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I just don't usually talk to people that way. \n\nI've been trying to think if there was anything else, like maybe at the grocery store? Sometimes the baggers put the canned goods right on top of the bread, and that used to just make me shake my head a bit, but I haven't yelled at anyone there. I haven't snapped at the tellers at the bank, even when they take forever with the deposit slips. It really was just that one awful, embarrassing outburst at the community center. \n\nI keep replaying it, you know? The way the plastic was hanging off the edge of the table, the way the kid was just tossing them down without a care in the world... and then the sound of my own voice echoing off those cinderblock walls. It was so loud. I didn't even know I had that kind of volume left in me at my age. And the look on his face... oh, it just makes my stomach tie up in knots just thinking about it. I've always prided myself on being a gentleman, on keeping a level head. Thirty-five years at the plant, you learn to let the little things slide, or you'd drive yourself crazy. But that day, over a tablecloth... I just lost it. And that's why I'm here. Because that's not me. It's just not me at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Well, now that you put it like that... let me think back. Weeks? No, definitely not weeks. I'm usually a very even-keeled fellow, like I've been telling you. But... if I really trace it back, trace back this whole feeling of being wound up so tight like a cheap watch, it has been going on for about a week. Yes, about a week exactly, now that I look at the calendar in my head. 

It all started last Tuesday. I remember because Tuesday is the day I always go down to the bank to handle my transactions. The First National over on Market Street. I've been going there since the late seventies, back when they actually knew your name when you walked in the door and the tellers would ask after your family. Anyway, I was just finishing up, stepping out the heavy glass front doors, maybe thinking about what I was going to pick up at the butcher shop for supper... and that's when it happened. There was a robbery, right there outside the bank. 

Right out on the pavement, doctor. I've lived in this town my whole life, worked thirty-five years at the plant, and I've never been caught up in anything like that. The shouting, the commotion... it was just pure chaos right there on the street. And I think... well, I know, ever since that afternoon, ever since that robbery outside the bank about a week ago, I just haven't been sitting right in my own skin. I've been feeling completely out of sorts, sensitive to every little noise, just... brewing, I suppose. 

So by the time Thursday rolled around and I was at the community center with that poor teenager and the tablecloths... I guess the pot was already boiling, so to speak. I just didn't realize it until the lid blew off. But yes, it's been about a week of this. Ever since the bank.

Like I said about the tablecloths, I like things neat and orderly. But that's not a change, doctor, that's just who I am. Have been for seventy-nine years. So no, nobody's come to me with any concerns about my personality. I'm just me. Same as always.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself becoming more easily frustrated or impatient, even in small things, that you might not have noticed before?\n\nPatient: Well, now, see... that's the thing. That's exactly why I'm sitting here in this chair right now, doctor. Because, normally, no. I'm a patient man. I raised a daughter, I worked thirty-five years on a factory floor with all sorts of characters, you have to be patient. But lately... well, specifically at the community center the other day, I just... I don't know what came over me. \n\nI was there early, like I always am on Thursdays for the card games, and they had these young volunteers setting up. High school kids, probably needing hours for some civics class or something. And this one boy, nice enough looking kid, but he was just throwing the tablecloths on the tables. Not smoothing them out, not lining up the edges with the corners of the tables. Just tossing them down like they were old rags. And they were crooked. So crooked. \n\nNormally, I'd just walk over, maybe make a little joke about how geometry wasn't his strong suit, and fix it myself. I've done it a hundred times. But this time... I just felt this heat rise up in my chest, right up into my neck. And before I even knew what I was doing, I was yelling at him. I mean, really raising my voice, telling him he was careless and disrespectful and if he couldn't do a simple job right he shouldn't be there at all. \n\nThe poor kid looked terrified, just froze right there with a plastic fork in his hand. And the whole room went quiet. You could have heard a pin drop on that linoleum floor. Mrs. Higgins, who runs the desk, she came hurrying over looking at me like I'd grown two heads. And I... I just didn't know what to say. I don't talk to people that way. I've never talked to people that way, not even when the guys at the plant would mess up a run on the line. It was so out of character, so... ugly. I felt awful about it, still do. I apologized, of course, but the damage was done. That's what really scared me, doctor. That quick temper, just snapping over a crooked piece of plastic. It just wasn't me.\n\nDoctor: Since that incident at the community center, have you had any other moments where you felt that sudden rush of anger or frustration over something small?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that. Since Thursday... Thursday afternoon I just went straight home. I didn't even stay for the rest of the setup or the card game, I was so rattled by my own behavior. I got in the Buick, drove back to the house—hit every single red light on Elm Street, which usually gets a little sigh out of me, but I was just so focused on what I'd done. \n\nHave I had another rush of anger? Well, not a full blow-up, no. I haven't yelled at anyone else. I did get a little... a little hot under the collar yesterday morning when the paperboy tossed the Sunday edition right into the middle of my wife's old rosebushes instead of on the porch where it belongs. I felt my jaw clench up real tight, and my heart started beating a little faster, and I muttered a few choice words under my breath while I was out there fishing it out of the thorns. But I didn't yell. I didn't go chasing down the street after the boy on his bicycle. \n\nBecause, honestly, doctor, that one time at the center... that was more than enough to scare me straight into this office. I mean, like I was trying to explain, what brought me in is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I don't usually talk to people that way. I've been turning it over and over in my head ever since. Why did I do it? Why a tablecloth? It’s just a cheap piece of plastic from the party store down the road. And the look on that poor kid's face... I keep seeing it every time I sit down in my armchair to watch the evening news. \n\nSo no, no other big outbursts, thank goodness, because I don't think my conscience could take it. But that one time was enough to let me know something isn't sitting right. Seventy-nine years of keeping my cool on the factory floor, and then... boom. Over a tablecloth. It's just not right, doctor, it really isn't. I just can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Has there been anything else recently, maybe even outside the community center, where you felt unusually frustrated or upset over small things being out of place or not done right?\n\nPatient: Well, outside the center... I mean, I mentioned the paperboy with the rosebushes, but... honestly, doctor, it really all comes back to that one moment. I know we keep circling back to it, but it's just weighing on me so heavily. Because, like I said, what brought me in here, sitting in this very office, is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I just don't usually talk to people that way. \n\nI've been trying to think if there was anything else, like maybe at the grocery store? Sometimes the baggers put the canned goods right on top of the bread, and that used to just make me shake my head a bit, but I haven't yelled at anyone there. I haven't snapped at the tellers at the bank, even when they take forever with the deposit slips. It really was just that one awful, embarrassing outburst at the community center. \n\nI keep replaying it, you know? The way the plastic was hanging off the edge of the table, the way the kid was just tossing them down without a care in the world... and then the sound of my own voice echoing off those cinderblock walls. It was so loud. I didn't even know I had that kind of volume left in me at my age. And the look on his face... oh, it just makes my stomach tie up in knots just thinking about it. I've always prided myself on being a gentleman, on keeping a level head. Thirty-five years at the plant, you learn to let the little things slide, or you'd drive yourself crazy. But that day, over a tablecloth... I just lost it. And that's why I'm here. Because that's not me. It's just not me at all.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Before this incident at the community center, had you been feeling more irritable, sad, or emotionally sensitive in the days or weeks leading up to it, even if only slightly?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you put it like that... let me think back. Weeks? No, definitely not weeks. I'm usually a very even-keeled fellow, like I've been telling you. But... if I really trace it back, trace back this whole feeling of being wound up so tight like a cheap watch, it has been going on for about a week. Yes, about a week exactly, now that I look at the calendar in my head. \n\nIt all started last Tuesday. I remember because Tuesday is the day I always go down to the bank to handle my transactions. The First National over on Market Street. I've been going there since the late seventies, back when they actually knew your name when you walked in the door and the tellers would ask after your family. Anyway, I was just finishing up, stepping out the heavy glass front doors, maybe thinking about what I was going to pick up at the butcher shop for supper... and that's when it happened. There was a robbery, right there outside the bank. \n\nRight out on the pavement, doctor. I've lived in this town my whole life, worked thirty-five years at the plant, and I've never been caught up in anything like that. The shouting, the commotion... it was just pure chaos right there on the street. And I think... well, I know, ever since that afternoon, ever since that robbery outside the bank about a week ago, I just haven't been sitting right in my own skin. I've been feeling completely out of sorts, sensitive to every little noise, just... brewing, I suppose. \n\nSo by the time Thursday rolled around and I was at the community center with that poor teenager and the tablecloths... I guess the pot was already boiling, so to speak. I just didn't realize it until the lid blew off. But yes, it's been about a week of this. Ever since the bank.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
On edge? Oh, doctor, that is putting it mildly. I don't think I've truly relaxed a single muscle in my body since that afternoon at the bank. It’s like I’m constantly waiting for the other shoe to drop, if you know what I mean. I'll just be sitting in my armchair, trying to watch Jeopardy, and I can't even focus on the trivia because my shoulders are practically hiked up to my ears. 

But it’s not just feeling tense in the living room. Let me tell you what happened just the other day. I was heading back over to the senior center—this was after the tablecloth incident, I was going over there to apologize to the director, because like I said, I'm a gentleman and I needed to make things right. So I'm walking up the pathway, right near the entrance. It's usually very peaceful there, they have those nice oak trees. And out on the main road, a car went by. One of those old beaters, you know the type, probably hasn't seen a mechanic in a decade. 

Well, the thing backfired. Just a loud *pop*. Now, normally, living in town, working in a noisy plant for thirty-five years, a car backfiring is nothing. You don't even blink. But doctor, when that noise hit my ears... I jumped so hard my chest actually hurt. I mean, a sharp, physical pain right in my ribs from how violently my whole body just jerked. I thought my heart was going to leap right out of my throat. 

And it didn't just end with the jump. I stood there on the concrete, and my hands... I looked down and my hands were just shaking. Trembling like leaves in a windstorm. I tried to put them in my pockets to steady them, but they just kept shaking for a good few minutes after. I actually had to go sit down on the little wooden bench they have out front by the flower beds just to catch my breath and wait for my hands to stop fluttering before I could even think about pulling the door handle to go inside. 

So, yes, I am definitely on edge. Every sudden noise, every loud voice, a door slamming too hard... it just shoots right through me now. I'm seventy-nine years old, my nerves just aren't built for this kind of constant, exhausting anticipation. It's like my body doesn't know the robbery is over.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.3, F43.22, F06.7, F06.4, F06.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... I suppose it's a bit of a story. You see, I've been helping out down at the community center for, oh, must be fifteen years now. Ever since I retired, really. Mostly I just help set up for the Sunday luncheons, bingo nights, things of that nature. I like to keep busy, you know? A man my age needs a reason to put his shoes on in the morning and get out of the house. \n\nAnyway, last week we were getting the hall ready, and there's this new kid, one of the younger volunteers. Good kid, I suppose, but just doesn't pay attention to the details, always looking at his phone.
